Message 
From the Municipal Planning and Development Coordinator 
I extend my heartfelt appreciation to: 
Mayor Jose M. Salting,Jr., whose political will and leadership were pivotal to finalizing the CLUP 
Atty. Rodolfo N. Ang, Jr. and Mr. Ranny M. Salting and the entire mayor's office, for their 
unwavering support during the most challenging phases 
My co-department heads, whose dedication was critical: Ms. Marlene S. Pangilinan, Ms. Teresita 
Alimurong, Ms. Agnes M. Lacanlale, Mr. William Cura, Engr. Reymart B. Macapagal, Ms. Cherrylyn 
M. Pascua, Ar. Harris S. Magtoto, Engr. Michael M. Lugma, Dr. Rommel P. Leal, Ms. Cherry Rose 
Guese, Ms. Rosario L. Campo, Engr. Oliver A. Maristela, and Ar. Anna Lynne N. Pineda 
The Sangguniang Bayan, led by Vice Mayor Leonardo C. Anunciacion and including Hon. Levy 
M. Tadeo, Mary Andrei Lacsamana, Hon. Semel Manipon, Hon. Rainier Rivera, Hon. Jayson 
Galang, Hon. Marino Rafael M. Morales, Hon. Alvin Sibal, Hon. Robin Mangiliman, IPMR Jun Jun 
Tolentino, and SK Federation Jake Casper Maglaqui 
Our Municipal Local Government Operations Officer (MLGOO),Mr. RomerT. Enaje 
DepEd Superintendents Ms. Loida L. Zamora and Mr. Roberto D. Santos 
The Bureau of Fire (FS Insp. Cedric T. Cruz) 
Bamban Chief of Police Leoncio P. Alcantara,Jr. 
The Civil Society Organizations, led by People's Council President Mr. Arnaiz S. Salas, Ms. 
u
Minerva Lumboy, Ms. Dianne Figueraa, former Vice Mayor Fred Lumboy, Mr. Loie Mendiola, 
Ms. Elena Salas, Mr. Roland Canlas, and all members of the People's Council 
The CLUP 2022-2031 will guide Bamban toward sustaining its land and water resources, preserving 
ecological integrity, and promoting balanced development. It positions Bamban as an emerging municipality 
poised for infrastructure growth and economic progress. More than a plan, it is a blueprint for a vibrant, 
resilient, and inclusive community-one that nurtures development while safeguarding the environment 
for generations to come.

The Local Government of Bamban extends its deepest gratitude to the many government and non-government organizations whose 
support, assistance, and contributions have been indispensable to the successful completion of the 2022-2031 Comprehensive Land 
Use Plan.
The Municipal Government of Bamban extends its heartfelt appreciation to Mr. Arnaiz S. Salas, President of the Bamban People's 
Council, along with his dedicated team of Civil Society Organizations and partner non-governmental organizations. Their invaluable 
insights and contributions during the consultation process were instrumental in the successful development of the Comprehensive Land 
Use Plan (CLUP) document.
Equal gratitude is extended to the various divisions and ofﬁces within the municipal government for their generous support—sharing 
their time, data, and expertise to help shape a comprehensive and well-informed plan.

   14 
Executive Summary 
The Municipality of Bamban's Enhanced Local Climate Change Action Plan (LCCAP) 2024-2028 
serves as a comprehensive framework for addressing climate change challenges through adaptation 
and mitigation strategies. It aligns with national climate policies such as the Climate Change Act of 
2009 (RA 9729), the Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Act of 2010 (RA 10121), and the 
Local Government Code of 1991 (RA 7160). This plan integrates climate resilience into local 
development goals, ensuring long-term ecological and socio-economic stability. 
Key Priorities and Objectives: 
1. Climate Adaptation and Resilience: Enhancing community resilience by protecting
vulnerable populations, fostering climate-responsive infrastructure, and securing food,
water, and energy resources.
2. Environmental Sustainability: Conserving biodiversity, restoring ecosystems, and
reducing greenhouse gas emissions.
3. Knowledge and Capacity Building: Strengthening local capacity through climate
education and sustainable development initiatives.
4. Inclusive Development: Promoting gender-sensitive, equitable policies for sustainable
growth.
Climate Vulnerability and Risk Assessment: 
The plan identifies climate-related hazards, including flooding, landslides, droughts, and volcanic 
lahar threats, emphasizing targeted interventions for disaster preparedness and recovery. It also 
highlights priority areas for environmental protection, infrastructure development, and socio-
economic resilience. 
Adaptation and Mitigation Strategies: 
Key projects include climate-resilient infrastructure development, ecological restoration, renewable 
energy expansion, disaster risk reduction programs, and community-based environmental 
management initiatives. 
Monitoring and Evaluation: 
A robust monitoring system ensures continuous evaluation of climate action progress, with 
adaptive management to meet emerging challenges. 
By implementing this plan, the Municipality of Bamban reaffirms its commitment to sustainable 
development, environmental stewardship, and the well-being of its constituents. 


===== PAGE 20 =====
Enhance Local Climate Change Action Plan 
   15 
Chapter 1 
1.
Introduction
1.1. 
Rationale
In accordance with the National Framework Strategy on Climate Change and the National Climate 
Change Action Plan (NCCAP), as mandated by Section 14 of Republic Act No. 9729, or the 
Climate Change Act of 2009, Local Government Units (LGUs) serve as the primary agencies 
responsible for formulating, planning, and implementing climate change action plans within their 
respective areas, consistent with the provisions of the Local Government Code. Furthermore, 
Section 11(2) of Republic Act No. 10121, also known as the Disaster Risk Reduction and 
Management Act of 2010, requires LGUs to integrate disaster risk reduction and climate change 
adaptation into local development plans, programs, and budgets as part of their sustainable 
development and poverty reduction strategies. This integration seeks to build resilience against 
climate change impacts by incorporating climate change analyses into policy-making, development 
planning, poverty reduction strategies, and other government processes. 
To align with these national policies, the Municipality of Bamban initiated the formulation of its 
Local Climate Change Action Plan (LCCAP). This process adhered to the guidelines outlined in 
DILG Memorandum Circulars, specifically MC 2014-135 (dated October 21, 2014), which details 
the LCCAP formulation process, and MC 2015-77 (dated July 21, 2015), which provides guidance 
on mainstreaming climate change adaptation (CCA) and disaster risk reduction (DRR) into local 
development planning. These directives led to the creation of the Municipal Local Climate Change 
Action Plan Core Team, formalized through Executive Order No. 27, Series of 2016. 
The Core Team adopted the following framework for Bamban’s LCCAP: 
1. Alignment with National Climate Strategy: Bamban’s LCCAP is aligned with the
National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP), which outlines the country’s agenda for
climate adaptation and mitigation from 2011 to 2028.
2. Addressing Climate Challenges: The plan seeks to tackle critical issues such as public
financing to reduce the vulnerability and risks faced by marginalized and poor communities.
3. Encouraging Private Sector Participation: It aims to create a supportive policy
environment that fosters private sector involvement, optimizing opportunities for climate
mitigation and promoting sustainable development.
4. Building Adaptive Capacities: The overarching goal of the framework is to enhance the
adaptive capacities of individuals and communities, particularly women and men,
strengthen the resilience of vulnerable groups and sectors, and safeguard natural ecosystems
against the impacts of climate change.
This comprehensive approach ensures that Bamban’s climate action efforts are both inclusive and 
aligned with national objectives, fostering sustainable and climate-resilient development for the 
municipality.


===== PAGE 21 =====
Enhance Local Climate Change Action Plan 
   16 
1.1.1. Legal Mandate 
The Philippine Constitution, through Section 1, Article II, declares that the State is committed to 
safeguarding and promoting the rights of its citizens to a balanced and healthful ecology, aligning 
this with the natural rhythm and harmony of the environment. This principle emphasizes the 
nation’s responsibility to protect the environment as an essential part of securing the welfare of its 
people. 
Recognizing the critical need for comprehensive action on climate change, the Philippines enacted 
Republic Act No. 9729, also known as the Climate Change Act of 2009. Section 2 of this 
landmark legislation underscores the State's policy to uphold and advance the people's right to a 
balanced and healthful ecology. The law stresses the need to address human needs while 
simultaneously safeguarding the natural environment to ensure sustainability for current and future 
generations. 
The Local Government Code of 1990 (Republic Act No. 7160) mandates Local Government Units 
(LGUs) to exercise their inherent powers, including police power, to help maintain ecological 
balance within their jurisdictions. Sections 2(a), 15, and 3(i) of this Code require LGUs to actively 
collaborate with the national government in managing and preserving the environment. This shared 
responsibility reflects the principle of subsidiarity, where local authorities are empowered to address 
ecological issues at the community level. 
Further strengthening LGUs' roles, Section 14 of the Climate Change Act, as amended by Republic 
Act No. 10174, designates LGUs as the frontline agencies for formulating, planning, and 
implementing climate change action plans. These responsibilities must align with the provisions of 
RA 7160, the National Framework Strategy on Climate Change (NFSCC), and the National 
Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP). This alignment ensures coherence between local actions 
and national strategies, facilitating a unified approach to mitigating climate change impacts and 
building adaptive capacities across all levels of governance. 
By prioritizing the integration of climate-responsive strategies and emphasizing local accountability, 
these legal frameworks empower LGUs to lead environmental protection efforts effectively, 
ensuring ecological integrity while fostering sustainable development. 
1.1.2. The National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) as Anchor of the 
Local Climate Change Action Plan 
The Municipality of Bamban Local Climate Change Action Plan (LCCAP) 2024-2028 is aligned 
with the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP), adopted in April 2010, which outlines 
the nation’s agenda for climate adaptation and mitigation from 2011 to 2028. The NCCAP was 
developed through multi-sectoral processes, ensuring that the concerns of various sectors were 
considered. It comprehensively addresses climate change challenges by prioritizing public financing 
for adaptation to reduce the vulnerability and risks faced by communities, particularly the 
marginalized and poor. Additionally, the NCCAP creates a policy environment that encourages 


===== PAGE 22 =====
Enhance Local Climate Change Action Plan 
   17 
private sector participation to optimize mitigation opportunities, fostering sustainable 
development. 
In line with this framework, the ultimate goal of Bamban’s LCCAP is to strengthen the adaptive 
capacities of women and men within their communities, enhance the resilience of vulnerable sectors 
and natural ecosystems to climate change, and maximize mitigation opportunities to achieve gender-
responsive and rights-based sustainable development. 
Unlike the national counterpart, which began in 2011, the Municipality of Bamban Local Climate 
Change Action Plan (LCCAP) 2024-2028 will commence its implementation upon its approval. 
The plan details specific programs and strategies for adaptation and mitigation from 2024 to 2028. 
It outlines key actions to enhance the adaptive capacity and resilience of both communities and 
natural ecosystems to climate change. Additionally, the plan incorporates the total economic value 
of natural resources, ensuring biodiversity conservation, and emphasizes the competitive advantages 
of recognizing the value of direct use, indirect use, options for future use, and non-use of 
environmental and natural resources. These efforts are aligned with the municipality's goal for 
sustainable development in both the short and long term. 
Aligned with the development concepts of the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP), 
the Bamban Local Climate Change Action Plan (LCCAP) outlines key priority areas and targeted 
outcomes to address the impacts of climate change effectively. These priorities are aimed at 
enhancing resilience, promoting sustainability, and fostering inclusive development across various 
sectors: 
1. Food Security - The plan aims to ensure the availability, stability, accessibility, and
affordability of safe and nutritious food despite the challenges posed by a changing climate.
2. Water Sufficiency - It focuses on enhancing the resilience of water resources and
infrastructure, promoting efficient water management, and ensuring the conservation and
quality of water supplies.
3. Environmental and Ecological Stability - The LCCAP seeks to protect and rehabilitate
critical ecosystems, restore essential ecological services, and achieve overall environmental
stability.
4. Human Security - The plan aims to reduce climate-related risks for vulnerable populations,
including women, children, senior citizens, persons with disabilities, and other
disadvantaged groups.
5. Climate-Friendly Industries and Services - Promotion of this sector is also prioritized,
with an emphasis on creating green jobs and encouraging sustainable consumption and
production practices.
6. Sustainable Energy - The plan prioritizes energy efficiency and conservation, the
development of renewable energy sources, the implementation of environmentally
sustainable transport systems, and the climate-proofing of energy infrastructure.
7. Knowledge and Capacity Development - The LCCAP emphasizes the enhancing
understanding of climate change science, building local and community capacities for
adaptation and disaster risk reduction, and establishing gender-sensitive knowledge
management systems that are accessible across all sectors at both national and local levels.


===== PAGE 23 =====
Enhance Local Climate Change Action Plan 
   18 
Together, these priorities reflect Bamban’s commitment to creating a resilient, sustainable, 
and inclusive future for its communities. 
1.2. 
Brief Municipal Profile 
1.2.1. Profile 
Table 1-1. Municipal Profile of Bamban, Tarlac 
The municipality of Bamban is a second class municipality in the Province of Tarlac. It is located in 
Central Luzon, bounded on the north by the municipality of Capas, Tarlac, on the east by the 
municipality of Concepcion (Tarlac) , on the south by the municipality of Mabalacat (Pampanga), 
on the west by the  municipality Botolan (Zambales), and on the southwest by the municipality of 
Porac (Pampanga).  
The municipality is politically subdivided intro 15 barangays, namely: Anupul, Banaba, Bangcu, 
Culubasa, Dela Cruz, La Paz, Lourdes, Malonzo, San Nicolas, San Pedro, San Rafael, San Roque, 
San Vicente, Santo Niño, Lumang Kalsada, and Virgen de los Remedios

the table below.

Revolution from Spanish conquerors 
1899 
Filipino-American revolution and proclamation of Bamban as the country’s revolutionary capital by

Japanese soldiers took over the sugar central and converted it as their barracks or detention camp.

and good will of civilians. 
Bambanenses joined guerilla movements such as the United States Armed Forces in the Far East (USAFFE) 
or the Hukbong Bayan Laban sa Hapon (HUKBALAHAP) 
Apung Nasiong Gamboa (also known as Commander Luna) lead the #45 of HUKBALAHAP 
1944 
Liberation and Post-Liberation Years 
The entire poblacion was devastated when the American fighter planes bombed houses, schools buildings, 
and the public market.  
The aftermath of the war lingered for an extended period, casting a shadow over the town. This prolonged 
period of uncertainty and instability prompted influential families to make a significant decision: relocating 
to the city in order to rebuild their lives and businesses. The allure of the city's resources, opportunities, 
and relative safety likely played a pivotal role in their decision-making process. This migration of prominent 
families further altered the social and economic landscape of both the town and the city, marking a 
significant transition in the wake of the war's devastation. 
1949 
 Atty. Igmedio Bolus established the first secondary school in Bamban, “Bamban Institute” 
1957 
The Holy Infant Jesus Academy was founded by Mr. Gaviola, however the management was then 
transferred to the Dominican Sisters and renamed the school into Sto. Niño Academy. 
1966 
Bamban’s First Public High School was established “The San Roque Experimental High School” now 
currently known as San Roque High School 
1972 
The detention of the Incumbent Mayor and subsequent replacement by the Vice Mayor caused significant 
disruption in the municipality's leadership. This abrupt change led to instability, uncertainty, and a loss of 
confidence among residents. The Vice Mayor faced challenges transitioning into the role of Mayor and 
struggled to fill the void left by their predecessor. This period of adjustment affected decision-making, 
policy implementation, and public trust. The absence of the Incumbent Mayor, who likely had strong 
community ties, resulted in a lack of leadership experience and political influence. Overall, the fallout 
highlighted the importance of stable leadership in local government for maintaining governance and 
stability. 
1991 
Mt. Pinatubo Eruption and Expiration of RP-US Bases Treaty 
the eruption of Mt. Pinatubo in 1991 devastated Bamban, affecting its economic, social, cultural, and 
political life. Lahar caused widespread destruction, submerging entire barangays and displacing residents. 
Two resettlement areas, Dap Dap and Mainang, were established to accommodate those affected. Schools 
were built in these areas to cater to the relocated children. 
The closure of the Clark Air Base due to the expiration of the RP-US Military Bases Treaty in the same year 
added to the economic woes, leaving many unemployed. However, the National Government later 
transformed the former military base into a special economic zone under the Clark Development 
Corporation. This led to the establishment of manufacturing plants, duty-free shops, and other businesses, 
creating employment opportunities for Bamban residents. Additionally, the Clark International Airport 
was developed into a major international gateway, further bolstering the local economy.

1.2.3. Demographic Profile 
1.2.3.1. Population 
Based on the 2020 PSA Census, Bamban's total population was recorded at 78,260. This represents 
a consistent increase, with an annual population growth rate (APGR) of 2.41%, calculated from the 
2015 Census figures, and 2.29% based on the 2010 Census. The rising growth rate suggests 
significant demographic shifts over the past decade, influenced by factors such as migration, urban 
development, and natural population increase. These trends reflect the evolving dynamics of 
Bamban's population and underscore the importance of careful planning to accommodate future 
growth. 
However, the 2022 Community-Based Monitoring System (CBMS) recorded a significant decrease 
in Bamban's population, dropping to 74,292, primarily due to the effects of the COVID-19 
pandemic. The pandemic led to increased mortality rates, economic hardship, and migration as 
residents sought safer or more stable living conditions. These factors, along with disruptions in birth 
rates and local development, contributed to the population decline. The sharp contrast between the 
town's earlier growth and the post-pandemic reduction highlights the substantial impact the health 
crisis had on Bamban’s demographics.

1.2.4. Social Services 
1.2.4.1. Health Facilities 
The data shows that there is a shortage of health infrastructure in the area. Currently, there are 2 Rural 
Health Units (RHUs), but 4 are needed, and this demand will rise to 5 RHUs in the future. Similarly, 
there are 10 Barangay Health Stations, but the demand is for 15, with a future need for 20 stations. 
Meanwhile, the 7.31 hectares of cemetery space is sufficient, with no additional space required for both 
current and future needs. This indicates that while health services need significant expansion, the cemetery 
capacity is adequate.

1.2.6. Local Economy 
Economic activities are traditionally classified into three sectors: primary, secondary, and tertiary. Activities 
falling under each sector that are present in the municipality are discussed below. 
1.2.6.1. Primary 
The Primary sector involves the extraction and production of natural resources. It includes activities related 
to agriculture, forestry, fishing, and mining.

1.2.6.2. Secondary 
The secondary sector includes economic activities which transform natural resources (collected through the 
primary sector) and other materials into higher-value products through manufacturing or processing.

1.3. 
Physical and Natural Background 
1.3.1. Topography 
1.3.1.1. Topographic Relief 
Bamban is situated at the southernmost tip of Tarlac Province. It is bordered to the north by Capas, to the 
east by Concepcion, to the south by Mabalacat City in Pampanga, and to the west by Botolan in Zambales. 
The town lies approximately 100 kilometers north of Manila and 32 kilometers from Tarlac City. 
1.3.1.2. Slope 
Bamban’s topography is characterized by extreme slope variation, from level to mountainous slopes. 25.72% 
of total land area have hilly to mountainous terrain, 23.41% have mountainous slopes, and 21.96% have level 
to undulating hills.  
Table 1-24. Slope Distribution in Bamban 
Slope Description

100% 
Source: NAMRIA 
1.3.1.3. Elevation 
Bamban's elevation ranges from 40 to 1,475 meters, with lower areas suited for agriculture and habitation 
due to their flat terrain. As the elevation increases, the landscape becomes hilly and mountainous, forming 
part of the Zambales Mountain Range. These higher elevations feature cooler temperatures, rugged 
terrain, and forested areas, playing an important role in the local ecosystem and watershed management.

1.3.1.4. Surface Drainage 
Bamban is traversed by two major rivers and their tributaries. The Sacobia River, located in the southern part of the municipality, is the primary river system, with 
its headwaters originating from Mt. Pinatubo. Similarly, the headwaters of the O'Donnell River System, which also flows from Mt. Pinatubo are found within 
Bamban. Since much of the municipality's land is classified as forest, it is also home to several small creeks that occasionally provide irrigation for agricultural areas.

1.3.2. Land Cover 
Land cover refers to the physical features present on the Earth's surface, both natural and human-made. It identifies what currently occupies the land, such as 
forests, grasslands, water bodies, or urban areas. Different land cover types include vegetation, built-up areas, water bodies, and barren land. Below are the types of 
land cover found in Bamban:

1.3.3. Soil Characteristics 
Majority total land area or 30.99% (10,753.67 has.)  of the area of Bamban have Angeles soil. This is followed by Tarlac clay loam with 18.72% (6,497.22has.) and 
Tarlac soil with 17.75% (6,160.84 has. 
1.3.3.1. Soil Suitability 
Bamban's soils are well-suited for growing a variety of crops, including rice, corn, mango, pineapple, vegetables, sugarcane, yam, and oil palm. The barangays of 
Anupul, San Vicente, and Santo Niño have soils ideal for cultivating a wide range of crops, including cacao, garlic, and legumes. In contrast, the barangays of 
Bangcu and Malonzo are the least favorable areas for crop production.

1.3.4. Climate 
Bamban has two (2) pronounced seasons, the wet season that starts from May up to lasts up to October and 
dry from the month of November to April. This type of climate is typically hot, humid, and tropical and is 
generally affected by the neighboring topography and prevalent wind direction that varies within the year. 
Tropical monsoon is carried into the area from the southeast in the month of May to September thereby 
causing heavy rainfall in the area. Most of the rainfalls are associated with typhoons. 
1.3.4.1. Temperature 
The average temperature in Bamban from 2010 to 2020 ranged from 25ºC (Dec. 2011) to 31ºC (May 2021). 
The highest recorded temperature was 37ºC (May 2021) and the lowest recorded temperature was 19º C 
(Jan. 2014) 
Figure 1-1. Max, min and average temperature in Bamban, 2010-2020 
Source: https://www.worldweatheronline.com/bamban-weather-averages/tarlac/ph.aspx 
The average high temperature in Bamban ranges from 30°C to 35°C, while the average monthly low 
temperature varies from 21°C to 26°C. The coldest months are from January to February. 
Figure 1-2. Monthly average temperature for Bamban 
Source: https://www.worldweatheronline.com/bamban-weather-averages/tarlac/ph.aspx 


===== PAGE 56 =====
Enhance Local Climate Change Action Plan            
 51 
1.3.4.2. Rainfall 
From 2010 to 2020, the highest average rainfall amount was at 599.4 millimeters (mm)  (July to Septempber 
2018). The lowest recorded average rainfall amount in Bamban was at 2.30 mm (January to March 2010).
Figure 1-3. Average rainfall for Bamban, 2010-2020 
 Source:https://www.worldweatheronline.com/bamban-weather-averages/tarlac/ph.aspx 
The average rainfall days in Bamban vary throughout the year from 9 to 28 days. The peak of precipitation 
typically happens in July with an average precipitation of 465.5 mm and average rainfall days of 30  days.
1.3.4.3. Wind Speed 
From 2010 to 2020, the average wind speed recorded ranged from 3.9 kilometers per hour with an average 
gust of 10.5 kilometers per hour and a maximum wind speed of 7.1 kilometers per hour (June 2010) to 15.6  
kilometers per hour with an average gust of 17.8 kilometers per hour and maximum wind speed of 10.5 
kilometers per hour (February 2020). 
Figure 1-4. Average rainfall for Bamban, 2010-2020 
Source:https://www.worldweatheronline.com/bamban-weather-averages/tarlac/ph.aspx 


===== PAGE 57 =====
Enhance Local Climate Change Action Plan            
 52 
1.3.4.4. Air Pressure 
Atmospheric or air pressure is equivalent to the weight of air above a given area on Earth’s surface or within 
its atmosphere. It is usually expressed in millibars (mb) or kilopascal (kPa). At sea level, the mean pressure is 
about 1,000 mb (100 kPa), varying by less than 5 percent from this value at any given location or time 
(Britannica, 2021). 
Over the last decade, the lowest atmospheric pressure recorded in Bamban was at 1,006.3 millibars (July 
2018 and August 2019) and the highest at 1,016 millibars (February 2019).  
Figure 1-5. Average air pressure in Bamban

1.3.4.5. Cloudiness 
Cloudiness or cloud cover refers to the extent to which the atmosphere is covered by cloud and is estimated 
in fractions or percent. Overcast refers to near 100% cloudiness while clear refers to near 0% cloudiness. The 
term cloudiness most often describes the horizontal extent of clouds, including all cloud altitudes as viewed 
from a weather station on the Earth or a satellite in space. 
From 2010 to 2020, the period with the least cloud cover in Bamban was in November 2015 with only 9 
percent cloudiness. The period with the most cloud cover was in August 2012 with 62 percent. 
Figure 1-7. Average cloud coverage in Bamban, 2010 – 2020 
Source: https://www.worldweatheronline.com/bamban-weather-averages/tarlac/ph.aspx 
1.3.4.6. Humidity 
Humidity is the amount of water vapor in the air. It is the most variable characteristic of the atmosphere 
and constitute a major factor in climate and weather (Britannica, 2013). 
The highest relative humidity of 90 percent was recorded in the municipality of Bamban in September 2019. 
The lowest average was at 57 percent which happened in May 2018. 
Figure 1-8. Average humidity in Bamban, 2010 – 2020 
Source: https://www.worldweatheronline.com/bamban-weather-averages/tarlac/ph.aspx 


===== PAGE 59 =====
Enhance Local Climate Change Action Plan            
 54 
1.3.4.7. UV Index 
The UV Index measures the strength of ultraviolet (UV) radiation from the sun, indicating the potential risk 
of skin and eye damage. The scale ranges from 0 (low risk) to 11+ (extreme risk), with higher values signaling 
greater danger and the need for more protection, such as sunscreen and protective clothing. The index is 
highest around midday and helps raise awareness about sun safety. 
Figure 1-9. UV radiation exposure category 
Source: https://www.who.int/uv/publications/en/UVIGuide.pdf 
Based on the 2010 to 2020 data, the UV Index in Bamban ranges from 6 to 7.  Based on the monthly average 
UV index of Bamban, the average UV index from November to June is at 7 and is at 6 for the rest of the year. 
Thus, comparing this to the WHO standards, as shown above, it falls under the ‘High’ exposure category. 
Figure 1-10. Monthly Average UV Index in Bamban 
Source: https://www.worldweatheronline.com/bamban-weather-averages/tarlac/ph.aspx

1.4.2. Guiding Principles 
Guided by the National Framework Strategy on Climate Change (2010-2022), the Municipality of 
Bamban adopted key principles in developing its Local Climate Change Action Plan (LCCAP) for 2019-
2025: 
1. Vision for Resilience: The Municipal Mayor envisions a climate-resilient Bamban characterized
by healthy, safe, prosperous, and self-reliant communities, supported by thriving and productive
ecosystems.
2. Building Resilience and Sustainability:
a.
Strengthen the adaptive capacity of communities and enhance the resilience of natural
ecosystems to climate change.
b. Optimize mitigation opportunities to promote sustainable development.
3. Precautionary Approach: Implement proactive measures to anticipate, prevent, or minimize the
causes and adverse effects of climate change. Scientific uncertainty should not delay action,
particularly when there is a threat of serious or irreversible damage.
4. Risk-Based Planning: Formulate strategies and activities based on the causes, magnitude, and
impacts of risks, ensuring decisions are risk-informed.
5. Science-Based Knowledge: Rely on scientific contributions, best practices, and local
circumstances to guide climate change initiatives.
6. Adaptation as a Priority:
a.
Emphasize adaptation as the core strategy, with mitigation actions pursued as
complementary where applicable.
b. Implement adaptation measures equitably, adhering to the principle of common but
differentiated responsibilities, with particular attention to the poor, women, children, and
other vulnerable groups.
7. Anticipatory Adaptation: Take preventive actions to address potential climate change impacts,
even when scientific information is incomplete.
8. Sustainability: Ensure that the LCCAP addresses human needs while preserving the quality of the
natural environment for present and future generations.
9. Complementation Across Sectors: Climate change initiatives in one sector must complement and
not hinder adaptation efforts in other sectors.
10. Multi-Stakeholder Engagement: Recognize the importance of partnerships and collaboration
among civil society, the private sector, and marginalized groups, ensuring inclusive participation in
climate change initiatives.
11. Private Sector Involvement: Encourage policies and incentive mechanisms that promote and
support private sector participation in achieving adaptation and mitigation objectives.

Enhance Local Climate Change Action Plan

Chapter 2 
2.
Vulnerability and Adaptation Assessment
This section outlines the findings of the Vulnerability and Adaptation Assessment (VAA) conducted for the municipality, which evaluates how climate change 
affects five key sectors: social, economic, physical/infrastructure, environmental, and institutional. These sectors are identified in the guidelines of the DILG 
Rationalized Local Planning System and the Local Planning Illustrative Guide, which help in preparing and updating the Comprehensive Development Plan. The 
climate data used in the assessment are based on projections from PAGASA (the Philippine Atmospheric, Geophysical and Astronomical Services Administration) 
and the local community’s own observations and experiences. Additionally, information from the Climate and Disaster Risk Assessment (CDRA)—which 
includes past disaster records, hazard inventories, exposure and sensitivity databases, and risk maps from the Mines and Geosciences Bureau (MGB)—was 
incorporated to develop climate change interventions and solutions. Essentially, this assessment integrates a range of climate-related data and local insights to 
understand vulnerabilities and propose adaptation strategies.
2.1. 
Climate Projections and Patterns of Change for Bamban 
2.1.1. Seasonal Temperature 
Box 2-1. Main Insight on Seasonal Temperature 
It will become hotter. Temperature will increase in every season from 2036 to 2065. 
The following table shows the seasonal temperature changes from 2036 to 2065 in Tarlac under the medium-range emission scenario (RCP 4.5). The seasonal 
temperature in the province of Tarlac is projected to change between 1.0°C to 1.9°C from 2006 to 2035 depending on the season. It is expected to reach a low of 
27.1°C in the DJF / northeast monsoon / amihan season and a high of 30.1°C in the MAM season.

2.2. 
Scope of the Potential Impacts of Climate Change 
To scope the potential impacts of climate change and disasters, impact chain analyses were conducted for (1) the impacts of increasing temperature, extreme 
temperature, and drought in all seasons, and (2) the impacts of increasing rainfall, extreme rainfall, and tropical cyclone for DJF and JJA for the same period. For 
both impacts, impact chains were prepared for the five systems of interest. 
2.2.1. Impacts of Increased Temperature 
Impacts of projected increase in temperature due to climate change include: 
1. Heat-related illness and death (Guo, Gasparrini, Armstrong, Li, & Tawatsupa, 2018)
2. Increased frequency and intensity of wildfires (Abatzoglou & Williams, 2016)


===== PAGE 65 =====
Enhance Local Climate Change Action Plan            
  60 
3. Reduced agricultural productivity and food security (Fischer, Knutti, & Schär, 2018)
4. Spread of infectious diseases (Gasparrini, et al., 2015) (Morin, Comrie, & Ernst, 2018)
5. Migration and displacement of populations (Black, et al., 2011)
6. Increased water scarcity and reduced access to safe drinking water (Hoque, Burgess, Karim, & Quaddus, 2020)
7. Damage to infrastructure and buildings from extreme weather events (IPCC, 2018)
8. Impacts on mental health and well-being (Berry, Bowen, Kjellstrom, & McMichael, 2010) (Clayton, Manning, Krygsman, & Speiser, 2017)
9. Economic losses and impacts on livelihoods (Hallegatte, et al., 2016)
10. Adverse impacts on vulnerable populations, including children, elderly, and low-income communities (Ebi & McGregor, 2008) (O'Neill, Ebi, &
Gronlund, 2018)
It should also be noted that increased temperature can have devastating impacts on Bamban and on the country’s food security, particularly on rice: 
1. Reduced rice yields: A study by Peng et al. (2004) found that a 1°C increase in minimum temperature during the rice grain-filling stage could result
in a 10% reduction in rice yields.
2. Increased incidence of pests and diseases: The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC, 2014) reports that rising temperatures can
increase the incidence of the rice blast fungus by up to 10% and the brown planthopper by up to 25%.
3. Water stress: Miah et al. (2017) report that a 1°C increase in temperature can increase rice water requirement by 4-6% and reduce rice yield by 6-7%.
4. Soil degradation: Singh et al. (2019) report that a 1°C increase in temperature can increase soil respiration by 10-20%, leading to increased soil organic
matter decomposition and soil degradation.
5. Changes in grain quality: Peng et al. (2004) report that a 1°C increase in night temperature during the rice grain-filling stage can reduce the head rice
yield by 6% and increase the percentage of chalky grains by 6%.
A mentioned, the average surface temperature in the Philippines has reached 1.6°C above pre-industrial levels (Berkley Earth, 2021). 
Not included in the main References section: 
Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change. (2014). Climate change 2014: impacts, adaptation, and vulnerability. Part A: global and sectoral aspects. Cambridge University Press. 
Kobayashi, K., Kim, H. Y., & Okada, M. (2010). Growth and yield responses of rice to global warming. Journal of agricultural meteorology, 66(4), 239-247. 
Miah, M. G., Ahmed, S., & Salam, M. A. (2017). Impacts of climate change on rice production and farmers’ adaptation in Bangladesh. Agriculture & Food Security, 6(1), 1-11. 
Peng, S., Huang, J., Sheehy, J. E., Laza, R. C., Visperas, R. M., Zhong, X., & Centeno, G. S. (2004). Rice yields decline with higher night temperature from global warming. Proceedings of the National 
Academy of Sciences, 101(27), 9971-9975. 
Singh, R. K., Reddy, K. R., & Reddy, V. R. (2019). Climate change impacts and adaptation strategies for sustainable rice production. Sustainability, 11(7), 2122.

2.3. 
Impacts of Increased Rainfall 
Projected increases in rainfall due to climate change can have various impacts such as the following: 
1. Flooding: Increases in rainfall can lead to more frequent and intense flooding, which can cause significant damage to infrastructure and property
(IPCC, 2018).
2. Landslides: Heavy rainfall can increase the risk of landslides, which can cause damage to buildings and infrastructure and pose a threat to human life
(Caine, 2019).
3. Waterborne diseases: Heavy rainfall can lead to contamination of water sources, increasing the risk of waterborne diseases such as cholera and typhoid
fever (Singh, Singh, & Singh, 2018).
4. Agricultural productivity: Changes in rainfall patterns can affect agricultural productivity, with some regions experiencing droughts and others
experiencing floods, both of which are possible in Bamban (Lobell, et al., 2018).
5. Public health risks: Increases in rainfall can lead to the spread of vector-borne diseases such as malaria and dengue fever, which are carried by insects
that thrive in wet conditions (Tanser, Sharp, & le Sueur, 2019).
6. Infrastructure damage: Heavy rainfall can damage roads, bridges, and other infrastructure, disrupting transportation and communication (IPCC,
2018).
7. Displacement of communities: Flooding and other impacts of heavy rainfall can force people to migrate from affected areas, leading to social and
economic disruption (Mertz, Halsnæs, Olesen, & Rasmussen, 2019).
8. Economic losses: Heavy rainfall and resulting flooding can lead to significant economic losses, particularly in low-lying areas (Hallegatte, Green,
Nicholls, & Corfee-Morlot, 2013).
9. Psychological impacts: Flooding and displacement can have psychological impacts on affected individuals, including stress, anxiety, and depression
(Lowe, Rhodes, & Zwiebach, 2013).
10. Social inequality: Vulnerable populations such as low-income households and indigenous communities may be disproportionately affected by the
impacts of heavy rainfall due to lack of access to resources and social discrimination (Liu, et al., 2018).
 It should also be noted that increased rainfall can likewise have devastating impacts on Bamban and on the country’s food security, particularly on rice: 
1. Increased flooding: Increased rainfall can lead to more frequent and severe floods, which can damage rice crops and reduce yields. The International
Rice Research Institute (IRRI, n/d) reports that just one week of flooding during the vegetative stage can reduce rice yields by 10-50%.
2. Waterlogging: Excess rainfall can also lead to waterlogging, which can reduce oxygen availability to rice roots and affect plant growth and
development. The IRRI (n/d) reports that even short periods of waterlogging can reduce rice yields by 30-50%.
3. Increased incidence of pests and diseases: Increased rainfall can create favorable conditions for the growth and spread of pests and diseases that can
damage rice crops. The IRRI (n/d) reports that high rainfall can increase the incidence of the rice stem borer by up to 40%.


===== PAGE 73 =====
Enhance Local Climate Change Action Plan            
  68 
4. Changes in grain quality: Excessive rainfall can lead to increased grain moisture content, which can reduce the quality of rice grains and increase the
risk of post-harvest losses. The IRRI (n/d) reports that rice grains with high moisture content can have lower milling recovery and higher
susceptibility to discoloration and mold growth.

2.5. 
Hazard Characterization 
2.5.1. Flood Hazard 
A total of 4,070.79 hectares, or 11.72% of the total land area of Bamban, is classified as highly susceptible to flooding. This significant portion of the municipality’s 
land area faces frequent flood risks, particularly during heavy rains and typhoons. As a result, this susceptibility imposes considerable limitations on the types of 
developments that can be undertaken in these flood-prone areas. 
For instance, high-density residential or commercial developments may not be feasible due to the increased risks of property damage and safety hazards. Instead, 
flood mitigation measures such as proper drainage systems, flood barriers, or retention basins would be essential prerequisites for any development in these zones. 
Moreover, the local government must carefully regulate land use in these areas, potentially restricting development to agriculture, parks, or other non-structural 
uses that can better withstand flooding. 
These limitations require that urban planning efforts in Bamban focus on mitigating flood risks while balancing economic development and environmental 
protection.
Box 2-3. Main Insight on Flood Hazard 
8,183.68 hectares or 23.58% of Bamban is susceptible to flooding. 
All barangays have areas which are susceptible to flooding. 
The following table shows the land area in hectares by flood susceptibility (very high, high, moderate, low, and none) and by barangay.

2.5.2. Rain-Induced Landslide Hazard 
15,933.67 hectares, or 45.09% of the total land area of Bamban, are classified as highly to very highly susceptible to landslide occurrences. These areas are primarily 
located in the hilly and mountainous regions of the municipality, where the steep slopes, loose soil, and heavy rainfall during the monsoon season increase the risk 
of landslides. 
The high susceptibility to landslides poses significant challenges for development in these areas. Infrastructure projects, such as roads, residential housing, and 
public facilities, must be carefully planned to avoid triggering landslides and ensure safety. Strict geotechnical assessments are necessary before any construction, 
and engineering solutions, such as slope stabilization, retaining walls, and proper drainage systems, may be required to mitigate the risk. 
Furthermore, land-use regulations in these zones may need to prioritize conservation and protection rather than intensive development. Agricultural activities, 
mining, and deforestation, which can destabilize slopes, should be strictly monitored or restricted. The local government, together with relevant agencies, must 
also implement early warning systems and evacuation plans to safeguard residents in these vulnerable areas, especially during typhoons and heavy rainfall. 
Balancing the preservation of natural landscapes and ecosystems with the safety and development needs of the population will be crucial in addressing the landslide 
risks in these susceptible zones. 
Box 2-4. Main Insight on Rain-Induced Landslide Hazard 
 26,521.48 hectares or 76.42% of Bamban is susceptible to rain-induced landslide hazard. 
Nine out of the 15 barangays are affected.  
The following table shows the land area in hectares by rain-induced landslide hazard (very high, high, moderate, low, and none) and by barangay.

2.5.5. Ground Shaking Hazard 
Box 2-7. Main Insight on Ground Shaking Hazard  
100% of Bamban is susceptible to ground shaking. 
About 60% is susceptible to a ground shaking of PEIS VII, while 40% by PEIS VIII or higher. 
Ground Shaking is the disruptive up, down, and sideways vibration of the ground during an earthquake. It can damage or collapse structures and may consequently 
cause hazards such as liquefaction and landslide. 
The entire municipality is susceptible to a Philippine Earthquake Intensity Scale (PEIS) Intensity VII ground shaking. This type of ground shaking is described in 
the table following the Ground Shaking Hazard Map.

 A sand boil is sand and water that come out onto the ground surface during an earthquake as a result of liquefaction at shallow depth. Source: https://earthquake.usgs.gov/learn/glossary

2.5.6. Liquefaction Hazard 
Box 2-8. Main Insight on Liquefaction Hazard 
 3,442.36 hectares or 9.92% of Bamban is susceptible to liquefaction. 
All barangays have areas that are susceptible.  
Liquefaction is the phenomenon wherein sediments, especially near bodies of water, behave like liquid similar to a quicksand. Mid to high-rise structures may be 
at high risk to liquefaction during an earthquake. Smaller structures may be affected as well.

2.9. 
Climate Change Influence Analysis 
2.9.1. Forest Ecosystem 
Currently, the total forest land area is 27,445.77 hectares. Under the proposed land use plan, this area is set to increase to 27,808.06 hectares, with the forest land 
divided into two categories: a forest reserve and a special use forest. The forest reserve will cover 22,123.63 hectares, while the special use forest will span 4,610.22 
hectares. 
However, despite the overall increase in the forest land area, there is a slight reduction of -2.59% in the amount of land designated as forest. This decrease reflects a 
shift in how the forested areas are categorized or utilized within the plan, possibly due to changes in land use priorities or adjustments to land classification. While 
the total area of forest land increases, this reduction indicates a smaller percentage of land being classified as traditional forest, with some areas potentially being 
reallocated for special uses or other purposes under the new land use plan. 
2.9.2. Agriculture Ecosystem 
The current agricultural land in the municipality, totaling 3,352 hectares, is proposed to be reduced by approximately 9.99%. This reduction reflects a strategic shift in land use 
planning aimed at enhancing economic viability while still recognizing the importance of agriculture in the community. 
To better manage and utilize the remaining agricultural land, it is proposed that it be classified into two distinct categories: Protection Agricultural Areas and 
Production Agricultural Areas. 
1. Protection Agricultural Areas: These zones are designated to conserve land that plays a critical role in maintaining the ecological balance, preserving 
biodiversity, and protecting vital agricultural resources. This classification helps ensure that the most valuable agricultural lands are safeguarded from urban 
development and other encroachments, allowing them to continue serving their environmental and community functions. 
2. Production Agricultural Areas: In contrast, these areas focus on land actively utilized for agricultural production. By emphasizing productive use, the 
municipality can support local farmers and agricultural businesses, fostering food security and promoting sustainable agricultural practices. This 
classification can also encourage the adoption of modern farming techniques, enhancing productivity and profitability for farmers. 
Additionally, the proposed zoning of approximately 340.55 hectares of agricultural land to other economically feasible uses signifies a proactive response to the 
changing economic landscape. By transitioning these areas to uses such as residential, commercial, or mixed-use developments, the municipality aims to attract 
investment, create jobs, and stimulate economic growth. This shift aligns with the broader goals of urban development while recognizing that not all agricultural 
land is equally suited for farming in the context of a rapidly evolving economy. 


===== PAGE 116 =====
Enhance Local Climate Change Action Plan            
  111 
Overall, this proposed reclassification and reduction in agricultural land signify a balanced approach to land use, aiming to protect essential agricultural resources 
while also promoting economic development that benefits the community as a whole. This dual strategy can help ensure that Bamban remains resilient and 
adaptable in the face of future challenges and opportunities.
2.9.3. Built-Up Ecosystem 
Some of the existing built-up areas do not follow the allowable land use. The commercial areas located in San Roque are unplanned developments in a protected 
area.

2.10. 
Exposure Information, Sensitivity Analysis and Evaluation of Adaptive Capacity 
2.10.1. Exposure Information 
2.10.1.1. Exposure Database 
The Exposure Database provides baseline information pertaining to the elements at risk. These elements refer to population, assets, structures, economic activities, 
and environmental resources which are located in areas exposed to potential impacts of climate change and disasters. The following tables show the exposure, 
sensitivity or vulnerability, and adaptive capacity of each barangay under each of the five systems of interest. 
Exposure is expressed as the area and/or monetary unit for social, economic, and environmental-related property. In terms of population, exposure is expressed as 
the number of affected individuals or households exposed to the climate stimulus. 
Sensitivity represents the intrinsic characteristics of the exposed elements to withstand the damaging effects of the expected climate stimulus. 
Vulnerability, on the other hand, is the degree to which a system is susceptible to, or unable to cope with, adverse effects of climate change, including climate 
variability and extremes. 
Finally, adaptive capacity is the ability of a system to adjust to climate change (including climate variability and extremes) to moderate potential damages, to take 
advantage of opportunities, or to cope with the consequences (HLURB, 2015).
2.10.2. General Exposure 
2.10.2.1. Population Exposure 
The following table shows the exposure attribute table for the population. Exposure data include residential areas (in hectares) and population. Sensitivity or 
vulnerability data include persons per hectare of total barangay area, housing made of light materials, and young and old dependents. Finally, adaptive capacity 
data include access to post disaster financing, Philhealth coverage, household financial capacities to relocate, government capacity to generate jobs, and government 
resources.

2.10.2.2. Urban Use Areas Exposure 
The following table shows the attribute table for urban use areas. Exposure data include the existing area of urban uses (in hectares). It should also include the 
replacement cost of buildings (PHP per sq.m.), but this data has yet to be collected. Sensitivity and vulnerability data include percentage of buildings with light 
materials, percentage of dilapidated buildings, percentage of buildings not using hazard-resistant design, and the absence of access to infrastructure-related hazard 
mitigation measures. Adaptive capacity data include capacity and willingness to retrofit or relocate, insurance coverage, available alternative sites, government 
resources, and LGU capacity to impose zoning regulations.

2.10.2.3. Natural Resource Based Production Areas Exposure 
The following table shows the attribute table for the exposure of Natural Resource Based Production Areas. Exposure data include agricultural area (ha), dominant 
crop, average yield (MT/ha), and forest (ha). Sensitivity or vulnerability data include percentage of farmers who attended climate field school, percentage of farmers 
who use sustainable farming techniques, percentage of farmers with access to hazard information, percentage of farms with irrigation coverage, and areas with 
water impoundment (hectares). Sensitivity and vulnerability are described as follows: Very High = >50%, High >30-50%, Moderate >15-30%, Low >5-15%, Very 
Low >2-5%, Residual 0-2%. Finally, adaptive capacity data include access to insurance, agricultural extension services of the LGU, early warning systems, alternative 
livelihood, and government resources.

2.10.2.4. Critical Point Facilities Exposure 
The following table shows the exposure attribute table for critical point facilities. Exposure data includes the existing land area of barangay halls, day care centers, 
elementary schools, high schools, health centers, and other facilities. Sensitivity or vulnerability data include construction materials used, existing condition of 
building/structure, and whether buildings or structures use hazard-resistant design. Adaptive capacity data include insurance coverage and local government 
resources for risk mitigation.

2.10.2.5. Lifeline Utilities Exposure 
 The following table shows the exposure attribute table for lifeline utilities. Exposure data should include the length of national, provincial, municipal, and barangay roads, water lines, power lines, 
and their replacement cost in PHP. However, consistent with the other systems of interest, the land area of the roads and utilities are shown instead. Sensitivity or vulnerability data include 
construction type, existing condition, and the use of hazard resistant design. Finally, adaptive capacity includes insurance coverage and available government resources.

2.10.3. Sensitivity Analysis 
Sensitivity analysis involves the determination of the degree of impact (either high, moderate, or low) of the three major impacts of climate change on the systems of interest. The 
impacts that are expected in the Philippines in the coming decades are: (1) increased temperature, (2) increased rainfall, and (3) sea level rise. Increased temperature and increased 
rainfall affects the entire municipality, but increased rainfall also increases flooding risk in the lowland areas. 
High degree of impact means that the estimated direct impacts in terms of number of fatalities, injuries and value of property damage will be disastrous given the extent of 
exposure and current sensitivity of the system. Medium- to long-term indirect impacts which may affect development processes will also be experienced. Significant costs are 
needed to return to pre-impact levels. 
Moderate degree of impact means that moderate direct impacts in terms of number of fatalities, injuries, and value of property damage are expected given the extent of exposure 
and current sensitivities of the system. Short- to medium-term indirect impacts which may affect development processes will also be experienced. Medium to low costs are needed 
to return to pre-impact levels within a short to medium time period. 
Low degree of impact means that the estimated direct and indirect impacts are low to negligible which can be felt within a short-term period. Minimal impacts to development 
processes and no significant costs are needed to return to pre-impact levels (HLURB, 2015). 
2.10.3.1. Degree of Impact of Increased Temperature, Extreme Temperature, or Drought

individuals in the short-term. Increased heat can cause 
illnesses to vulnerable individuals such as children, the 
elderly, and pregnant women. Increased heat can also 
cause stress or stroke to those working outdoors: 
farmers, fisherfolks, etc.

Every 1°C in warming can decrease rice production by 
3.2% (Zhao, Liu, Piao, & Asseng, 2017). in another 
study, a 1°C increase in nighttime temperature 
decreased rice production by 10% (Peng, et al., 2004). 
Increase in temperature can have similar impacts on 
other crops.

critical point facilities as urban use areas.

creating uneven surfaces or potholes. This can cause 
accidents and delays in services such as emergency 
response. Climate change may adversely affect 
electricity supply adequacy by reducing generation and 
transmission capacity while simultaneously increasing 
electricity demand. Extreme heat and drought can 
impair power generation capacity, limit the current-
carrying capacity (ampacity) of transmission lines, and 
increase peak electricity loads (Bartos, et al., 2016). 
Water pipes exposed to extreme temperature can 
rupture and leak.

for the municipality (decrease in food security). 
Critical Point Facilities 
  
Increased and prolonged rainfall can prevent the 
government from delivering public services such as 
healthcare, education, social welfare, and others. This 
can also lead to more damaging floods, affecting both 
structures and property inside (or around) those 
structures.

Lifeline Utilities 
Strong typhoons can directly damage electrical posts and 
power lines or through felled trees. Increased and 
prolonged flooding can damage water facilities and can 
contaminate water sources for domestic use, affecting 
health. More intense flooding can also erode soils and 
damage roads as well as bridges, cutting off communities 
from basic needs and social services. 
2.10.4. Evaluation of Adaptive Capacity 
After determining the degree of impact on various systems, the next step is to evaluate the adaptive capacity of the systems of interest. Establishing the flexibility of a system 
requires information on its current absorptive capacity, production practices, design standards, and remaining economic life, among others (HLURB, 2015). 
Low Adaptive Capacity means that the system is not flexible to accommodate changes in the climate. Addressing the impacts will be costly and the LGU and property owners 
will require external assistance to address the impacts. 
Moderate Adaptive Capacity means that addressing the impacts will require significant costs but it is still within the capacity of a system to adapt to potential impacts. It can 
accommodate the cost for adapting and mitigating impacts using its resources. 
High Adaptive Capacity means that the system is able to accommodate changes in climate. There are adaptation measures in place to address impacts.

2.10.5. Vulnerability and Risk Assessment 
2.10.5.1. Vulnerability Level 
Vulnerability is the degree to which a system is susceptible to, or unable to cope with, the adverse effects of climate change. The degree of impact will be compared with the level 
of adaptive capacity to determine the level of vulnerability. For example, a system that will experience a high degree of impact and has a low adaptive capacity can be considered 
highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change.
2.10.5.2. Vulnerability to Increased Temperature 
The vulnerability to increased temperature of Bamban’s population is moderate; urban use areas is low; Natural Resource Based Production Areas is moderate; and critical point 
facilities and lifeline utilities are low.

2.10.6. Interventions 
There are eight broad categories of adaptation approaches or options which may be the basis for interventions: (1) bear the losses, (2) share losses through insurance, 
relief, rehabilitation, and reconstruction, (3) modify the threat, (4) prevent the effects, (5) change the use, (6) change the location, (7) research, and (8) encourage 
behavioral change through regulation and through information, education, and communication (IEC) campaigns (UNEP-IES, 1998).

Chapter 3 
3.
Local Climate Change Action Plan Objectives
3.1. 
Vision and Development Goal
Bamban: A gateway for economic growth in the Province of Tarlac, inhabited by empowered, disciplined, 
healthy, and Godly citizenry who thrive in a resilient, peaceful, and livable community, nestled in a clean 
and sustainable environment prospering in a progressive, competitive, and diversified economy, supported 
by accessible, reliable, and disaster-resilient infrastructure, and led by highly-motivated public servants who 
are committed to transparency, responsiveness, and good governance. 
3.2. 
Mission 
The Local Government of Bamban is committed to develop effective, efficient and pro-active governance 
and ensure the general welfare of the citizenry 
3.3. 
LCCAP Goals and Objectives 
3.3.1. Goals 
The Municipality of Bamban’s Local Climate Change Action Plan (LCCAP) for 2024-2028 is designed to 
address the pressing challenges of climate change while fostering sustainable development. Its goals are 
strategically aligned with key sectors to enhance resilience, promote environmental sustainability, and 
support the well-being of the community. 
3.3.1.1. Social Sector 
1.
Vision: Healthy Citizenry
Goal 1:  Promote a healthy life 
•
Project: Repair, rehabilitation and expansion of RHU 2 equipping it to handle first aid emergency situations
•
Project: Construction of additional Barangay Health Centers
•
Project: Agriculture Center, for the development of agricultural produce
•
Policy: Protect Agriculture Lands, and enable farmers to produce healthier meal options for the people
Goal 2:  Ensure availability and sustainable management of Water and Sanitation with improved nutrition for all ages. 
•
Project: Delineate condition of existing water and sanitation system
•
Project: Municipal Water Supply and Sanitation Master Plan

3.3.2. Objectives 
The objectives of the Local Climate Change Action Plan 2024-2025 of the Municipality reflect the work 
priorities defined in the National Climate Change Action Plan 2011-2028 on food security, environmental 
and ecological stability, human security and knowledge and capacity building. These objectives include: 
1. To ensure availability, stability, accessibility and affordability of safe and healthy food amidst climate
change
2. To ensure ecosystem resiliency and environmental stability through protection and rehabilitation of
critical ecosystems and restoration of ecological services
3. To reduce the risks of human especially the vulnerable sectors to climate change and disasters
4. To enhance the knowledge of the community on climate change, capacity for climate change
adaptation, mitigation and disaster risk reduction and;
5. To establish a gendered climate change knowledge management accessible to all sectors at the local
levels

Enhance Local Climate Change Action Plan            
  283 
Chapter 5 
5.
Monitoring and Evaluation
Monitoring and evaluation (M&E) are critical components of the Municipality of Bamban’s Local Climate 
Change Action Plan (LCCAP) 2024-2028. These processes ensure that climate adaptation strategies are 
effectively implemented, targets are met, and adjustments are made based on lessons learned. Guided by 
Bamban’s vision of becoming a resilient, peaceful, and livable community supported by disaster-resilient 
infrastructure and good governance, M&E will support evidence-based decision-making and adaptive 
management. 
5.1. Objectives of Monitoring and Evaluation 
1. Assess Plan Implementation: Evaluate the progress of LCCAP activities against set targets.
2. Enhance Decision-Making: Provide timely and relevant data to inform adjustments and
improvements.
3. Ensure Accountability: Hold implementing agencies responsible for their roles in achieving
climate resilience.
4. Promote Transparency: Share M&E findings with stakeholders and the public to build trust and
cooperation.
5. Foster Adaptation and Learning: Identify gaps, challenges, and best practices for future climate
change initiatives.
5.2. Monitoring Framework
5.2.1. Data Collection and Recording 
Bamban will establish a climate data management system to collect, store, and manage data from various 
sectors, including agriculture, infrastructure, and disaster management. Key data sources will include: 
●
Municipal and barangay-level climate and disaster reports
●
Agricultural productivity and land-use data
●
Infrastructure damage and rehabilitation records
5.2.2. Performance Indicators 
Key performance indicators (KPIs) will align with the LCCAP’s strategic goals, covering the following areas: 
●
Resilience and Adaptation: Number of climate-resilient infrastructure projects completed
●
Environmental Sustainability: Area of reforested or rehabilitated land
●
Disaster Preparedness: Percentage of barangays with updated disaster preparedness plans
●
Socio-Economic Well-being: Number of livelihoods supported through climate-smart agriculture
initiatives


===== PAGE 289 =====
Enhance Local Climate Change Action Plan            
  284 
5.2.3. Reporting Frequency 
●
Annual Monitoring: Conducted at the end of each fiscal year to assess progress and inform budget
planning.
●
Mid-Term Evaluation (2026): Comprehensive evaluation to review efficiency, effectiveness, and
impact.
●
End-Term Evaluation (2028): A final evaluation to assess overall plan achievements and
recommend future actions.
5.3.
Evaluation Approach 
5.3.1. The Evaluation Team Composition will include representatives from: 
●
Municipal Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Council (MDRRMC)
●
Municipal Development Council (MDC)
●
Relevant local government departments (Agriculture, Environment, Planning)
●
Barangay leaders and civil society organizations
5.3.2. Evaluation Criteria: 
●
Relevance: Alignment of initiatives with climate risks and community needs.
●
Effectiveness: Achievement of project objectives and targets.
●
Efficiency: Cost-effectiveness of resources utilized.
●
Impact: Long-term improvements in climate resilience and socio-economic well-being.
●
Sustainability: Potential for continued benefits beyond the LCCAP implementation period.
5.4.
Institutional Support and Capacity Building 
Recognizing Bamban’s limited resources and manpower, the municipality will strengthen its institutional 
structures by enhancing the capacity of the MDRRMC, Barangay Disaster Risk Reduction and 
Management Councils (BDRRMCs), and other special bodies. Regular training, workshops, and technical 
assistance will ensure that staff are equipped with skills for effective M&E implementation. 
5.5. 
Budget Considerations 
Given resource constraints, Bamban will integrate M&E activities into its annual budget planning. Priority 
will be given to high-impact projects, ensuring that limited funds are allocated efficiently. External funding 
and partnerships with government agencies, NGOs, and the private sector will also be pursued. 


===== PAGE 290 =====
Enhance Local Climate Change Action Plan            
  285 
5.6. Summary 
By institutionalizing a robust Monitoring and Evaluation System, Bamban will ensure that its LCCAP 2024-
2028 remains adaptive, relevant, and impactful. This system will enable the local government to assess 
climate change responses, address emerging challenges, and continuously improve climate resilience 
efforts—advancing the municipality’s vision of a sustainable, disaster-resilient, and empowered community.
